I’ll think I know what someone is about to say and I jump in before they finish. Sometimes I end up cutting them off, or I answer the wrong thing because I misunderstood where they were going. It’s frustrating because I don’t always mean to be rude, but it comes out before I stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, with friends, or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it by setting. Like I’ll forget to pay bills, miss appointments, or not follow through on errands, and I’ve also failed to return emails for interviews. That’s part of why I came in—it’s starting to cost me real opportunities.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll sit down to do something like update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then one little thing pulls me away. It could be a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task. Then I’m completely off track before I even realize it happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. If I have to sit through something like an interview, a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body like I need to move. I’ll shift around, want to get up, or need to do something with my hands, otherwise I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that a lot in conversations, especially interviews. I’ll think I know where the person is going with a question and jump in before they’ve finished, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered something they weren’t actually asking. It’s embarrassing, and I don’t always catch myself until after I’ve already done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in your daily conversations, not just in interviews?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens in regular conversations too. I’ll think I know what someone is about to say and I jump in before they finish. Sometimes I end up cutting them off, or I answer the wrong thing because I misunderstood where they were going. It’s frustrating because I don’t always mean to be rude, but it comes out before I stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, with friends, or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it by setting. I mean, I’ve noticed it in different places, but I don’t know if it’s the same level at home or with friends or when I’m by myself. It’s not something I’ve really tracked that closely.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t clearly connected it to specific problems with friends or family. I know there are moments where I probably come across as annoying or I interrupt, but I don’t know if it’s caused major relationship problems.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back to elementary school. Teachers would write things like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find assignments. So I guess I started noticing it pretty young, even if I didn’t really understand it then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it got seriously worse about a year and a couple months ago, around when things fell apart at my biotech job. And it didn’t really let up after I got fired. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up reliably. So I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I need to move forward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep forgetting basic adult responsibilities. Like, I’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t return emails for interviews, or I’ll miss appointments. I also start errands and then just don’t follow through. It’s been happening enough that it’s costing me actual opportunities, and I feel like I need help figuring out what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with forgetting or not following through at work or in school, or is it mainly in your personal life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my personal life right now, since I’m not working or in school at the moment, but it’s definitely affecting work-related stuff too. Like I’ll forget to pay bills, miss appointments, or not follow through on errands, and I’ve also failed to return emails for interviews. That’s part of why I came in—it’s starting to cost me real opportunities.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll sit down to do something like update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then one little thing pulls me away. It could be a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task. Then I’m completely off track before I even realize it happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. If I have to sit through something like an interview, a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body like I need to move. I’ll shift around, want to get up, or need to do something with my hands, otherwise I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that a lot in conversations, especially interviews. I’ll think I know where the person is going with a question and jump in before they’ve finished, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered something they weren’t actually asking. It’s embarrassing, and I don’t always catch myself until after I’ve already done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in your daily conversations, not just in interviews?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens in regular conversations too. I’ll think I know what someone is about to say and I jump in before they finish. Sometimes I end up cutting them off, or I answer the wrong thing because I misunderstood where they were going. It’s frustrating because I don’t always mean to be rude, but it comes out before I stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, with friends, or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it by setting. I mean, I’ve noticed it in different places, but I don’t know if it’s the same level at home or with friends or when I’m by myself. It’s not something I’ve really tracked that closely.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t clearly connected it to specific problems with friends or family. I know there are moments where I probably come across as annoying or I interrupt, but I don’t know if it’s caused major relationship problems.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back to elementary school. Teachers would write things like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find assignments. So I guess I started noticing it pretty young, even if I didn’t really understand it then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it got seriously worse about a year and a couple months ago, around when things fell apart at my biotech job. And it didn’t really let up after I got fired. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. Even now without a job, I’m kind of moving all day. I’ll start laundry, then jump to emails, then reorganize tabs on my computer, then pace around, then start dinner or something. It feels like I can’t really let myself stop, but somehow a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity is as problematic for you as your inattention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. Maybe not always in the same way, but it causes problems. Like in conversations or interviews, I’ll jump in before the other person is done because I think I know what they’re asking, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me seem less prepared than I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Maybe not always in the same way, but it causes problems. Like in conversations or interviews, I’ll jump in before the other person is done because I think I know what they’re asking, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me seem less prepared than I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember it going back to elementary school. Teachers would write that I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find my assignments. So it was definitely there before I was 12.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity been present for more than 6 months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been seriously affecting me for about 1 year and 2 months, starting around when things fell apart at the biotech job and continuing after I was fired.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep forgetting basic adult responsibilities. Like, I’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t return emails for interviews, or I’ll miss appointments. I also start errands and then just don’t follow through. Like I’ll forget to pay bills, miss appointments, or not follow through on errands, and I’ve also failed to return emails for interviews. If I have to sit through something like an interview, a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body like I need to move. I do that a lot in conversations, especially interviews. I’ll think I know where the person is going with a question and jump in before they’ve finished, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered something they weren’t actually asking. The really serious part has been going on for about a year and two months now. It’s embarrassing, and I don’t always catch myself until after I’ve already done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in your daily conversations, not just in interviews?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens in regular conversations too. I’ll think I know what someone is about to say and I jump in before they finish. Sometimes I end up cutting them off, or I answer the wrong thing because I misunderstood where they were going. It’s frustrating because I don’t always mean to be rude, but it comes out before I stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, with friends, or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it by setting. I mean, I’ve noticed it in different places, but I don’t know if it’s the same level at home or with friends or when I’m by myself. It’s not something I’ve really tracked that closely.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t clearly connected it to specific problems with friends or family. I know there are moments where I probably come across as annoying or I interrupt, but I don’t know if it’s caused major relationship problems.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back to elementary school. Teachers would write things like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find assignments. So I guess I started noticing it pretty young, even if I didn’t really understand it then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it got seriously worse about a year and a couple months ago, around when things fell apart at my biotech job. And it didn’t really let up after I got fired. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up reliably. So I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I need to move forward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even now without a job, I’m kind of moving all day. I’ll start laundry, then jump to emails, then reorganize tabs on my computer, then pace around, then start dinner or something. It feels like I can’t really let myself stop, but somehow a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity is as problematic for you as your inattention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. Maybe not always in the same way, but it causes problems. Like in conversations or interviews, I’ll jump in before the other person is done because I think I know what they’re asking, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong thing. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up reliably. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up reliably. So I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I need to move forward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yes. I haven’t clearly connected it to specific problems with friends or family. I know there are moments where I probably come across as annoying or I interrupt, but I don’t know if it’s caused major relationship problems.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back to elementary school. Teachers would write things like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find assignments. So I guess I started noticing it pretty young, even if I didn’t really understand it then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of forgetfulness, distraction, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it got seriously worse about a year and a couple months ago, around when things fell apart at my biotech job. And it didn’t really let up after I got fired. Since then it’s felt pretty consistent and hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your daily life or ability to function independently?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it feels like I haven’t been able to keep my life moving. I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up reliably. So I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I need to move forward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even now without a job, I’m kind of moving all day. I’ll start laundry, then jump to emails, then reorganize tabs on my computer, then pace around, then start dinner or something. It feels like I can’t really let myself stop, but somehow a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity is as problematic for you as your inattention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. Maybe not always in the same way, but it causes problems. Like in conversations or interviews, I’ll jump in before the other person is done because I think I know what they’re asking, and then I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me seem less prepared than I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember it going back to elementary school. Teachers would write that I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets, rushing through directions, talking out of turn, and my desk was so messy I couldn’t find my assignments. So it was definitely there before I was 12.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity been present for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. The really serious part has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started around when things fell apart at my biotech job, and it’s kept going even after I was fired.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to maintain relationships or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Since I got fired, it’s been really hard to keep moving forward. I’ve missed follow-ups after interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about because I couldn’t keep up with it reliably. So right now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even manage the basic steps I need to take to get my life back on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms of forgetfulness, distraction, restlessness, and impulsivity in all areas of your life, including when you're alone, or are they mainly noticed by others in social or professional settings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out. I notice it when I’m by myself too, like trying to get things done or keep track of what I’m doing, but other people notice it more when I’m around them. So it’s not only a social or work thing, but I can’t always tell how much is happening until it causes a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at home when you are alone?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m alone at home, I forget basic things I’m supposed to handle, like paying bills or following up on emails after interviews.
